\subsection{Listed companies}
KOSPI- and KOSDAQ-listed corporations, identified by both an OpenDART corp-code (eight-digit) and a KRX
stock code (six-digit) --- e.g.\ Samsung Electronics (corp \texttt{00126380}, stock \texttt{005930}) and
SK~Hynix. They file the periodic business reports that carry the K-IFRS financial statements; extracted in
full.

\subsection{Other disclosure-obligated companies}
Non-listed companies subject to external-audit and registration disclosure, identified by corp-code only.
Their periodic reports carry K-IFRS statements through the same \texttt{fnlttXbrl} path and are extracted
wherever an XBRL package is available.

\subsection{Investment funds}
Collective investment vehicles, which file fund-specific reports. Discovery is reachable through OpenDART;
extraction is \emph{not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Large-holding and ownership filers}
Investors crossing the 5\,\% large-holding threshold, and executives and major shareholders reporting their
holdings. They file ownership disclosures rather than financial statements. Discovery only --- extraction is
\emph{not implemented yet}.

\section{Forms and statement types}\label{sec:dart-forms}

OpenDART groups disclosures into several families (Table~\ref{tab:dart-forms}); \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts
the periodic disclosures --- the carriers of the financial statements --- and reaches the rest for discovery
as the adapter matures. Each family is detailed below, followed by the statement types those reports carry.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{DART disclosure families and their support in \texttt{EDGAR.jl}.}
\label{tab:dart-forms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.43\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Family ($\to$ subsection)} & \textbf{Documents} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
Periodic disclosures & Business Report (annual), Half-year Report, Quarterly Reports & statements extracted \\
Major-event \& issuance & Major-Matters Report; securities registration statements & \emph{not yet} \\
Ownership disclosures & 5\,\% large-holding report; executive / major-shareholder holding report & \emph{not yet} \\
Other disclosures & fair-disclosure and other periodic filings & \emph{not yet} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Periodic disclosures}
The business report (\krcjk{사업보고서}{\emph{saeop bogoseo}}, the annual report), the half-year report and the
quarterly reports --- DART's analogue of the 10-K/10-Q, carrying the K-IFRS financial statements. Extracted
in full via the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package.

\subsection{Major-event and issuance disclosures}
The major-matters report and securities registration statements, filed on material events and securities
offerings. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Ownership disclosures}
The 5\,\% large-holding report and the executive / major-shareholder holding report. \emph{Not implemented
yet}.

\subsection{Other disclosures}
Fair-disclosure and other periodic filings. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Statement types: K-IFRS by reuse}\label{sec:dart-kifrs}

DART is the case the two-axis design (\S\ref{sec:two-axes}) was built for: a new \emph{jurisdiction} that
brings \textbf{no new taxonomy}. Because K-IFRS \emph{is} IFRS, a Korean filer's primary statements are
tagged with bare \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts (\texttt{ifrs-full:Assets}, \texttt{ifrs-full:Revenue},
\texttt{ifrs-full:ProfitLoss}, \texttt{ifrs-full:CashFlowsFromUsedInOperatingActivities}, \ldots), which
the existing \texttt{ifrs-full} vocabulary (shared with ESEF, Companies House and EDINET's IFRS filers)
already classifies. \textbf{No \texttt{vocab\_krgaap.jl} was needed.}

This was confirmed on two real FY2023 annual reports (\texttt{fnlttXbrl} packages): Samsung Electronics
(\texttt{5{,}997} facts) and SK~Hynix (\texttt{6{,}306} facts). In both, roughly \textbf{80\,\% of
concepts are bare \texttt{ifrs-full}} (Samsung \texttt{4{,}735}, SK~Hynix \texttt{5{,}072}); the remainder
are the filer's own \texttt{entity\ldots} extensions ($\sim$11\,\%) and Korea-specific \texttt{dart} /
\texttt{dart-gcd} concepts ($\sim$10\,\%). The balance sheet, income statement and cash-flow statement all
classify through the shared vocabulary, and \texttt{select\_statement} round-trips on both filers (Samsung
BS~1757 / IS~409 / CF~227; SK~Hynix BS~2043 / IS~126 / CF~216); a filing flows through the same pipeline as
every other system (\S\ref{sec:dart-workflows}).

\noindent Even the Korea-specific lines classify without a vocabulary. \textbf{Operating income}
(\krcjk{영업이익}{\emph{yeongeop iik}}) is tagged \texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} --- a \texttt{dart}-namespace
concept, not \texttt{ifrs-full} --- yet \texttt{facts(f;\ classify=true)} still places it on the income
statement, because the classification engine reads the filing's \emph{presentation-linkbase statement
roles}, not concept anchors alone (\S\ref{sys:extraction}). So a national extension is classified by
\emph{where} it sits in the income-statement role, and no \texttt{vocab\_krgaap.jl} is needed at all ---
verified on Samsung, where \texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} and numerous other \texttt{dart}/\texttt{entity}
lines (e.g.\ cash-flow proceeds) are correctly bucketed. This is a stronger result than EDINET's, which
needed a \texttt{jpigp\_cor} vocabulary addition (\S\ref{sys:edinet}): for K-IFRS, reuse plus role-based
classification suffices outright.

\section{Discovery and the corp-code resolver}

Unlike EDINET (date-based), OpenDART discovery is \textbf{by company}, because DART publishes a clean
identity index. \texttt{discover(OpenDARTApi();\ corp\_code|stock\_code,\ bgn\_de,\ end\_de)} lists a
company's periodic disclosures from \texttt{list.json} and returns handles, keeping the annual business
report (\krcjk{사업보고서}{\emph{saeop bogoseo}}) and pointing each handle at the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} financial-statement package
(\texttt{reprt\_code\ =\ "11011"}). A free \texttt{crtfc\_key} is required, set via
\texttt{set\_credentials(DART();\ api\_key)} or the \texttt{DART\_API\_KEY} / \texttt{OPENDART\_API\_KEY}
environment variable. (OpenDART authenticates with the key as a \emph{query parameter}, not a header, so
unlike the other keyed systems \texttt{system\_headers(::DART)} is empty and the key is appended to the
URL.)

Every other OpenDART endpoint is keyed by the \texttt{corp\_code}, not the public ticker, so resolution
goes through \texttt{corpCode.xml} --- a ZIP holding one \texttt{CORPCODE.xml} that maps
\texttt{corp\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ stock\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ name} for \emph{every} registered
Korean entity ($\sim$118{,}000; $\sim$4{,}000 listed). \texttt{dart\_corp\_code("005930")} resolves a
ticker (or a name) to its \texttt{corp\_code}, parsing that index in a single linear pass
(\texttt{0.84\,s} for 118{,}371 rows) and caching it (\S\ref{sec:dart-caching}).

\paragraph{Bilingual labels.} DART's label linkbase is \emph{split per language} ---
\texttt{\ldots\_lab-ko.xml} (Korean) and \texttt{\ldots\_lab-en.xml} (English), each single-language ---
unlike EDINET's one bilingual file. So \texttt{label\_map(f;\ lang)} (and \texttt{facts(f;\ labels=true,\
lang)}) selects the file for the requested language: \texttt{lang="en"} yields English labels,
\texttt{lang="ko"} Korean. On Samsung this gives 1{,}742 labels in each language (e.g.\
\texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} \(\to\) ``Operating income(loss)'' / ``\krcjk{영업이익(손실)}{yeongeop iik (sonsil)}'').
The \texttt{lang} keyword threads through the shared linkbase fetcher but is consulted only by systems
with per-language label files (just DART today); every other system ignores it, so their behaviour is
unchanged.

\section{Caching: slow endpoints and the per-system policy}\label{sec:dart-caching}

OpenDART's \emph{document} endpoints are \textbf{pathologically slow} --- measured at $\sim$12\,KB/s, so a
single \texttt{fnlttXbrl} filing takes minutes and \texttt{corpCode.xml} (\texttt{3.4\,MB}) clocked
\textbf{293\,s} --- while its metadata \texttt{list.json} answers in $\sim$1\,s. This is the forcing case
for the \textbf{per-system cache policy} (\S\ref{sec:caching}): a single global cache mode is too coarse
when DART is minutes-slow but the SEC and ESEF are fast. \texttt{set\_cache\_policy(DART();\
cache\ =\ :persistent)} makes \emph{only} DART persist its downloads across sessions (each slow file paid
once, ever) while everything else keeps the ephemeral default. It is the recommended setting for any
sustained DART work.

The corp-code index has its own renewal rule, because the
\texttt{corp\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ stock\_code} mapping is stable: rather than the global 24\,h
\texttt{cache\_ttl} (which would re-pay the multi-minute download daily), it is rebuilt only when older
than a jurisdiction-specific backstop --- \textbf{30 days} by default, tunable with
\texttt{dart\_index\_ttl!(days)}. As an immediate self-heal for newly-listed companies, a \emph{ticker}
lookup that misses re-downloads the index once and retries; \texttt{dart\_corp\_code(q;\ refresh\ =\
true)} forces a fresh pull on demand.

\section{Validation: independent cross-checks}\label{sec:dart-oracle}

Korea is the hardest jurisdiction to validate independently. filings.xbrl.org does \textbf{not} carry KR,
so there is no Arelle xBRL-JSON oracle (unlike ESEF/UKSEF, \S\ref{sec:validation}), and
\texttt{company\_facts}/AlphaVantage are US-centric. The independent reference that \emph{does} cover Korea
is \textbf{Yahoo Finance}, whose KRX coverage uses the suffixes \texttt{.KS} (KOSPI) and \texttt{.KQ}
(KOSDAQ) --- e.g.\ Samsung \texttt{005930.KS}, SK~Hynix \texttt{000660.KS}. The do-it-yourself oracle
\texttt{src/sources/yahoo\_oracle.jl} compares EDGAR.jl's revenue, net income, assets and equity for the
latest annual report against Yahoo's --- an \emph{independent data pipeline}, the same arms-length check it
applies to every other jurisdiction. One DART subtlety the wiring handles: the consolidated face line is
not left undimensioned but tagged on the \texttt{ConsolidatedAndSeparateFinancialStatementsAxis}
(\texttt{ConsolidatedMember}), so those face facts are normalised to undimensioned for the shared metric
picker.

Yahoo covers only headline totals, so the \textbf{statement of changes in equity} --- a movements-by-equity-component
table --- needs a fact-level, dimension-aware reference. That is the \textbf{local Arelle oracle}
(\texttt{src/sources/arelle\_local\_oracle.jl}, \S\ref{sec:validation}): it runs Arelle itself on the
downloaded \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package and compares \emph{every} fact, keyed by concept~+~period~+~full
dimension set, so the per-component SCE cells are checked individually. On Samsung FY2023 it confirms
\textbf{5997/5997} of EDGAR.jl's facts against Arelle --- all five statements, the SCE included
(159/159) --- comparing values \emph{raw} (Arelle reports the as-stored value; EDGAR.jl's classified value
flips sign for \texttt{negatedLabel}/cash-outflow concepts).

OpenDART additionally re-serves each filing's parsed accounts as \texttt{fnlttSinglAcntAll.json} (the FSS's
own parse). It is a handy sanity peek --- \texttt{account\_id} values such as \texttt{ifrs-full\_Assets} map
straight onto our concepts --- but because it comes from the \emph{same} disclosure system as the filing it
is not an independent reference, so EDGAR.jl ships no comparison harness for it.

\section{Workflow Use Case Examples}\label{sec:dart-workflows}

Practical DART workflows --- a few lines each. The live path resolves a KRX stock code to its corp-code,
fetches the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package, then classifies and labels through the shared pipeline; more
examples will follow.

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> set_credentials(DART(); api_key = ENV["DART_API_KEY"])    # free OpenDART crtfc_key
julia> hs = discover(OpenDARTApi(); stock_code = "005930",       # Samsung; resolves to corp_code 00126380
                     bgn_de = "20240101", end_de = "20241231");
julia> f  = fetch_filing(first(hs));                             # the fnlttXbrl package
julia> rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true);          # K-IFRS facts, classified
julia> select_statement(rows, "BalanceSheet")                    # BS / IS / CF as usual
julia> label_map(f; lang = "en")                                 # English labels (lang="ko" for Korean)
\end{lstlisting}

% ===========================================================================
% ===  VALIDATION  ==========================================================
% ===========================================================================
\begin{subappendices}
\section{Credentials}\label{sec:dart-credentials}

OpenDART requires a \emph{free} \texttt{crtfc\_key} (register on the OpenDART portal). Set it per session, or
from the environment:

\begin{lstlisting}
set_credentials(DART(); api_key = ENV["DART_API_KEY"])
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The same key authenticates discovery, the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package download and the
\texttt{corpCode.xml} resolver.
\end{subappendices}

\chapter{China \textemdash{} CSRC / CNINFO}
\label{sys:china}

\emph{Status: planned --- placeholder chapter, not yet researched or implemented.}

China is the strongest \textbf{multi-system-per-country} case: disclosure is split across the
\textbf{CSRC} (listed companies, surfaced through the \href{http://www.cninfo.com.cn}{CNINFO} portal and
the Shanghai/Shenzhen exchange sites), the \textbf{MOF}/\textbf{SASAC} channel (state-owned enterprises,
largely internal), and \textbf{AMAC} (funds). It also has the lowest XBRL-retrieval maturity of the major
markets --- the exchange portals expose no clean machine API --- and a large standalone \textbf{CAS}
(Chinese Accounting Standards) national-GAAP vocabulary that would need its own taxonomy mapping. The
repository survey, access mechanics and taxonomy work are deferred; this chapter is a scaffold so the
structure is in place when the work begins.

\chapter{India \textemdash{} NSE, BSE and MCA21}
\label{sys:india}

India is being added as the \textbf{sixth} \texttt{FilingSystem}. Unlike the single national regulator-API
of EDINET or DART, Indian financial disclosure is \textbf{multi-channel}: the same company's numbers surface
through the corporate registrar (\textbf{MCA21}) and through every stock exchange the company lists on
(\textbf{NSE}, \textbf{BSE}). Which channel to build on is therefore the central design decision for the
adapter --- so this chapter opens, unusually, with a \emph{Repositories} survey (\S\ref{sec:india-repos}).
Listed-company financials are filed in \textbf{Ind-AS} --- the IFRS-converged Indian Accounting Standards ---
under a national XBRL taxonomy published by the exchanges, and classify through a \textbf{dedicated}
\texttt{vocab\_indas.jl} keyed on the \texttt{in-bse-fin} namespace (the same by-namespace national-vocabulary
pattern as Japan and the UK; \S\ref{sec:india-taxonomy}). Identity is the company's \textbf{ISIN}
(\texttt{EntityId(:isin,\ "INE002A01018")}), with the NSE ticker symbol and BSE scrip code as secondary
schemes.

\emph{Status:} the seam (I0), the \textbf{offline parse} (I1) and \textbf{live NSE discovery} (I2) are
\textbf{implemented and tested}. \texttt{discover(NSEFilings();\ symbol=\ldots)} lists a company's filings
and \texttt{fetch\_filing} downloads the Ind-AS XBRL (\S\ref{sec:india-retrieve}); \texttt{facts(f)} extracts
its Ind-AS facts and \texttt{narratives(f)} its section text --- verified end-to-end against real filings
(Reliance, TCS, Maruti, \ldots). The dedicated \texttt{vocab\_indas.jl} \emph{classification} (I3) and
human-readable \emph{labels} plus the same-document BSE oracle (I4) are the remaining phases. This
\textbf{supersedes} the earlier ``India MCA21 --- paid'' note in the portability survey: MCA21 (the
registrar) is indeed paid, but that is the \emph{wrong} channel --- the \textbf{exchanges publish the
results XBRL keyless}.

\paragraph{Facts \emph{and} section text.} An NSE filing yields both. \texttt{facts(f)} returns the numeric
Ind-AS statements (the \texttt{in-bse-fin} concepts); \texttt{narratives(f)} (\S\ref{sec:api-narratives})
returns the filing's \textbf{explanatory text-blocks} --- the Regulation~33 notes tagged with the
\texttt{\ldots{}ExplanatoryTextBlock} suffix --- with \textbf{no India-specific code}, because the shared
text layer already recognises that suffix (exactly as for ESEF and EDINET). Reliance's FY2023--24 filing
exposes three: \emph{Notes on Financial Results}, \emph{Notes on Assets and Liabilities} and \emph{Notes on
Segments} (e.g.\ ``Q4~FY24 Segment Revenue includes Rs~24{,}119~crore GST recovered''). This is unlike DART,
whose \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package is numeric-only (\S\ref{sys:dart}). One current limit: until the standard
BSE label linkbase is wired (I4), a narrative's \texttt{section\_title} is the raw concept name rather than a
human label --- the prose \emph{bodies} are complete.

\section{Repositories}\label{sec:india-repos}

There is no single ``Indian EDGAR.'' A listed Indian company's financial statements are lodged, in the same
Ind-AS XBRL, with more than one repository, under the market regulator's disclosure rules (\textbf{SEBI}'s
Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements, Regulation~33 --- quarterly and annual financial results).
The three repositories differ sharply in \emph{access}, which is what decides where \texttt{EDGAR.jl}
fetches from (Table~\ref{tab:india-repos}).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Indian financial-disclosure repositories and their accessibility.}
\label{tab:india-repos}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.24\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Repository} & \textbf{What it is} & \textbf{Access} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
\textbf{NSE} & National Stock Exchange corporate-filings portal; the results XBRL of every NSE-listed company & \textbf{Keyless} JSON API (cookie-gated); direct Ind-AS \texttt{.xml} URLs & \textbf{primary channel} (planned) \\
\textbf{BSE} & Bombay Stock Exchange filings portal; \emph{publisher} of the \texttt{in-bse-fin} taxonomy & \textbf{Keyless} JSON API (endpoint not yet mapped) & second channel / cross-check (planned) \\
\textbf{MCA21} & Ministry of Corporate Affairs statutory registry; every company's annual AOC-4 XBRL & \textbf{Paid}, per-document; no bulk API & \emph{out of scope} (wrong door) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{NSE --- the primary channel (keyless)}
The National Stock Exchange exposes a corporate-filings \textbf{financial-results} endpoint that behaves
exactly like the discovery-to-XBRL seam of EDINET and DART, but with \textbf{no API key} --- only a browser
\emph{cookie bootstrap}. A query by ticker symbol returns a JSON array, one object per results filing, and
\textbf{each object carries a direct, keyless URL to the Ind-AS XBRL instance} (plus the ISIN, the
consolidated/standalone flag, the audited flag and the financial year). This is the repository the adapter
is built on. The access flow is three requests:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=term]
# 1. Bootstrap cookies from the homepage (a browser User-Agent is required).
% curl -c cookies.txt -A "$UA" https://www.nseindia.com/

# 2. Discover a company's results filings (keyless JSON; send the cookies + a Referer).
% curl -b cookies.txt -A "$UA" -H "Referer: https://www.nseindia.com/" \
    "https://www.nseindia.com/api/corporates-financial-results?index=equities&symbol=RELIANCE&period=Annual"

# 3. Fetch the Ind-AS XBRL instance named in each record's `xbrl` field.
% curl -b cookies.txt -A "$UA" -H "Referer: https://www.nseindia.com/" \
    https://nsearchives.nseindia.com/corporate/xbrl/INDAS_104634_1106038_22042024074704.xml
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent Each discovery record looks like the following (fields abridged); the \texttt{xbrl} URL is the
parse target, and \texttt{isin} is the identity key:

\begin{lstlisting}[style=term]
{
  "companyName":   "Reliance Industries Limited",
  "isin":          "INE002A01018",
  "consolidated":  "Consolidated",          # or "Standalone" -- filers lodge both
  "audited":       "Audited",
  "financialYear": "01-Apr-2023 To 31-Mar-2024",
  "period":        "Annual",
  "indAs":         "Ind-AS New",
  "xbrl":          "https://nsearchives.nseindia.com/corporate/xbrl/INDAS_104634_1106038_22042024074704.xml"
}
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent Two practical notes. First, the cookie bootstrap is mandatory --- the API returns nothing to a
cookieless client, and NSE runs bot detection, so a realistic \texttt{User-Agent} and the \texttt{Referer}
header matter; the session cookies expire and must be refreshed (refresh-on-failure, \emph{not} a retry
loop). Second, an NSE filing is a \textbf{single \texttt{.xml} instance} --- the linkbases are \emph{not}
bundled (the standard-taxonomy-only shape also seen at Companies House), so classification leans on the
presentation roles plus the canonical concept anchors rather than a bundled presentation linkbase.

\subsection{BSE --- the taxonomy publisher and second channel}
The Bombay Stock Exchange hosts the same Regulation~33 results filings and, importantly, is the
\textbf{publisher of the taxonomy} the instances are written in (the \texttt{in-bse-fin} namespace,
\S\ref{sec:india-taxonomy}). BSE also exposes a \textbf{keyless} JSON API (a probe returns HTTP~200), which
makes it the natural authoritative \emph{second} channel: because both exchanges publish the same company's
same filing, one can be cross-checked against the other as a same-document oracle. The exact
announcement/results endpoint and its six-digit \emph{scrip-code} addressing are \textbf{not yet mapped};
NSE alone is sufficient for the initial adapter, and BSE is deferred to the cross-check work.

\subsection{MCA21 --- the registrar (out of scope)}
MCA21 is the Ministry of Corporate Affairs' statutory registry, where \emph{every} incorporated company ---
listed or not --- files its annual return and financial statements (the AOC-4 XBRL form for the classes
required to tag). It is the most \emph{complete} repository, but access is \textbf{paid and per-document}
(document-purchase fees, no bulk or keyless API), which makes it the wrong door for XBRL extraction. The
listed-company financials that MCA21 holds are the same statements the exchanges publish for free, so
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} takes the exchange channel and treats MCA21 as out of scope.

\subsection{Taxonomy --- \texttt{in-bse-fin}, IFRS names under a national namespace}\label{sec:india-taxonomy}
A real NSE instance (Reliance, consolidated, FY ending 31~March~2024) resolves the taxonomy question that
governs classification. \textbf{Every} reporting concept sits in a \emph{single national namespace},
\texttt{in-bse-fin} (\texttt{http://www.bseindia.com/xbrl/fin/2020-03-31/in-bse-fin}, BSE's ``Financial
Results'' taxonomy) --- there are \textbf{zero} bare \texttt{ifrs-full} tags. But the concept
\emph{local names are IFRS-identical}: \texttt{in-bse-fin:Assets},
\texttt{in-bse-fin:AdjustmentsForDepreciationAndAmortisationExpense},
\texttt{in-bse-fin:AmountOfItemThatWillBeReclassifiedToProfitAndLoss} are the verbatim \texttt{ifrs-full}
element names, merely re-namespaced.

This is the \textbf{inverse of Korea}. K-IFRS filings tag under \emph{bare} \texttt{ifrs-full}, so DART
needed no vocabulary and no rewriting (\S\ref{sec:dart-kifrs}); India tags the \emph{same} IFRS concepts
under a national prefix. Because \texttt{EDGAR.jl} classifies by \textbf{namespace, not prefix}, an
\texttt{in-bse-fin} concept will not match the \texttt{ifrs-full} vocabulary as-is. \texttt{EDGAR.jl}
therefore classifies India through a \textbf{dedicated \texttt{vocab\_indas.jl}} keyed on the
\texttt{in-bse-fin} namespace --- the same by-namespace national-vocabulary pattern as Japan
(\texttt{vocab\_jpgaap}) and the UK (\texttt{vocab\_ukgaap}), rather than rewriting concepts onto
\texttt{ifrs-full}. Because the local names are IFRS-identical, that vocabulary is largely the IFRS concept
set re-keyed under \texttt{in-bse-fin}, plus the genuinely India-specific extensions. (This is phase~I3; the
offline parse and raw \texttt{facts} already work without it.)

\medskip\noindent \textbf{Two national namespaces, one pattern.} The Reliance example above is
\texttt{in-bse-fin} (the old \texttt{corporates-financial-results} feed). SEBI's current \textbf{Integrated
Filing} framework re-namespaces the \emph{same} IFRS-derived concepts once more, under \texttt{in-capmkt}
(\texttt{www.sebi.gov.in/xbrl/2025-01-31/in-capmkt}) --- so India has \emph{two} national taxonomy families,
not one. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} classifies each by its namespace: \texttt{vocab\_indas} for \texttt{in-bse-fin},
a parallel \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} for \texttt{in-capmkt} (which also anchors the insurers' premium-based
revenue accounts). The per-sector templates of both families are Table~\ref{tab:india-formats}
(\S\ref{sec:india-formats}).

\section{Entities covered}\label{sec:india-entities}

Unlike EDINET or DART --- one regulator, one inclusion rule --- ``which companies can I reach?'' has a
\textbf{different answer per repository}, because the three channels cover different universes
(Table~\ref{tab:india-entities}). All three receive the \emph{same} Ind-AS results filing under SEBI
Reg.~33; they differ in \emph{which} entities are obliged to lodge with them. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} builds on the
exchange channels, so its covered set is \textbf{listed issuers}; the registrar's far larger all-companies
universe is out of scope (paid).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Indian repositories cover different entity universes (hence a per-repository inclusion rule).}
\label{tab:india-entities}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.13\textwidth}p{0.37\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Repository} & \textbf{Inclusion criterion} & \textbf{Universe (order of mag.)} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
\textbf{NSE} & equity listed on the National Stock Exchange (files Reg.~33 results) & $\sim$2{,}000 issuers & \textbf{primary} --- extracted \\
\textbf{BSE} & equity listed on the Bombay Stock Exchange --- a \emph{superset}: includes BSE-only SMEs and smaller issuers & $\sim$5{,}000+ issuers & second channel (planned) \\
\textbf{MCA21} & \emph{every} incorporated company --- listed or unlisted, public or private & $>$1 million active & \emph{out of scope} (paid) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{NSE-listed and BSE-listed issuers}
The exchange channels cover \textbf{listed equity issuers} filing quarterly and annual financial results
under SEBI Reg.~33. Most large companies are listed on \emph{both} exchanges (dual-listed), so NSE and BSE
overlap heavily at the top; BSE's roll is the larger and messier (many BSE-only SMEs and small issuers that
NSE does not carry). \texttt{EDGAR.jl} takes NSE as the primary channel and BSE as the authoritative second
channel and cross-check (\S\ref{sec:india-repos}); an entity is identified by its ISIN, with the NSE symbol
and BSE scrip code as secondary schemes. Each filing is lodged in both \textbf{standalone} and
\textbf{consolidated} form (the results API flags which); \texttt{EDGAR.jl} picks per request, defaulting to
consolidated.

\subsection{MCA21 --- all incorporated companies (out of scope)}
MCA21 additionally holds the annual accounts of \emph{every} registered company --- unlisted and private
included --- a universe orders of magnitude larger (India has over a million active companies). It is the
only channel that reaches \emph{unlisted} entities, but access is paid and per-document
(\S\ref{sec:india-repos}), so \texttt{EDGAR.jl} does not use it; the listed-company statements it holds are
the same ones the exchanges publish for free.

\paragraph{Boundary.} The results API surveyed here covers \textbf{equity issuers} (Reg.~33). Other
SEBI-regulated vehicles that also publish financial results --- REITs, InvITs, mutual funds --- file under
\emph{separate} regulations and endpoints, not yet surveyed; they are outside the current India scope.

\section{Forms and statement types}\label{sec:india-forms}

\textbf{SEBI} --- the Securities and Exchange Board of India --- is India's capital-markets regulator, the
analogue of the U.S.\ SEC (statutory under the SEBI Act, 1992). Listed companies disclose to the exchanges
(NSE, BSE) chiefly under the \textbf{SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations,
2015} (``LODR''), with the takeover code (SAST, 2011), the insider-trading rules (PIT, 2015) and the
capital-issue regulations (ICDR, 2018) governing the rest. Disclosure is keyed to these \emph{regulations},
not to a catalogue of numbered forms as at the SEC: a listed company files a whole \emph{universe} of
documents (Table~\ref{tab:india-forms}), of which \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts the one that is XBRL-tagged
financial data --- the periodic \textbf{financial results} --- and reaches the others for discovery as the
adapter (and the PDF-extraction track) matures. Each disclosure class is detailed in a subsection below; the
financial-statement structure of the periodic results --- the per-company-class \emph{format templates}
(\S\ref{sec:india-formats}), the taxonomies (\S\ref{sec:india-taxonomies}) and the statement types and their
classification (\S\ref{sec:india-statements}) --- follows at the end.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{The Indian exchange-disclosure landscape --- each class detailed in a subsection below.}
\label{tab:india-forms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.24\textwidth}p{0.38\textwidth}p{0.12\textwidth}p{0.11\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Class ($\to$ subsection)} & \textbf{Documents (regulation)} & \textbf{Format} & \textbf{In EDGAR.jl} \\
\midrule
Periodic reports & quarterly \& annual results (LODR 33); annual report (LODR 34) & XBRL / PDF & results extracted \\
Material-event disclosures & board outcomes, price-sensitive events (LODR 30); corporate actions & PDF / metadata & \emph{not yet} \\
Shareholding disclosures & quarterly shareholding pattern (LODR 31) & XBRL & \emph{not yet} \\
Ownership \& insider & insider trades (PIT~7), substantial acquisition (SAST) & metadata / PDF & \emph{not yet} \\
Tender offers \& mergers & open offers (SAST), schemes of arrangement & PDF & \emph{not yet} \\
Issuance disclosures & IPO / rights offer documents (ICDR), buy-backs & PDF & \emph{not yet} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Periodic reports}\label{sec:india-periodic}
The scheduled financial filings. The \textbf{financial results} (LODR Regulation~33) --- filed
\textbf{quarterly} (Q1--Q3) and \textbf{annually} --- are India's analogue of the 10-Q/10-K and the
\emph{only} XBRL-tagged financial filing, the one \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts. They go to \emph{both} NSE and
BSE, each period in a \textbf{standalone} and a \textbf{consolidated} version (the API flags which;
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} defaults to consolidated). The annual filing --- the Q4 results carrying the full-year
column --- holds the audited annual statements; \emph{what} it contains depends on the company class (its
\emph{format template}, \S\ref{sec:india-formats}), and the statements are detailed in
\S\ref{sec:india-statements}. The fuller \textbf{annual report} (LODR Regulation~34) --- the complete
accounts with the notes and the statement of changes in equity that the condensed results omit --- is also a
periodic filing, but a PDF, and a candidate for the PDF-extraction track rather than the XBRL path.

\subsection{Material-event disclosures}\label{sec:india-material-events}
Event-driven, ad-hoc disclosures of material corporate developments (LODR Regulation~30) --- entirely
different in nature from the scheduled periodic reports, and the exact analogue of the SEC's Material-Event
Disclosures (Form 8-K, \S\ref{sec:edgar-material-events}). A company must promptly inform the exchanges of
deemed-material events (acquisitions, board-meeting outcomes, fund-raising, changes of directors / KMP /
auditors, credit-rating changes, insolvency proceedings, \ldots) and of other events its materiality policy
deems material, each as an \textbf{announcement with a PDF attachment} (the exchange exposes the feed keyless
--- confirmed live: each record carries a timestamp and an attachment URL). \textbf{Corporate actions} ---
dividends, bonus issues, stock splits and book-closure / ex-dates --- belong here too (a board-declared
dividend is itself a Regulation~30 event), and are additionally published as a structured keyless feed of
dates and ratios. All of this is PDF and metadata, not XBRL financial data --- \emph{not yet extracted}; the
announcement PDFs are candidates for the PDF-extraction track.

\subsection{Shareholding disclosures}\label{sec:india-shareholding}
The quarterly \textbf{shareholding pattern} (LODR Regulation~31) --- the promoter / public / institutional
ownership breakdown --- is itself filed as \textbf{XBRL}, on a template separate from the financial results.
\emph{Not yet handled}; a natural second XBRL target after the financial results.

\subsection{Ownership and insider disclosures}\label{sec:india-ownership}
Changes in \emph{control and insider} holdings, under two regimes. The insider-trading rules (PIT, 2015)
require promoters, directors and designated persons to disclose their trades --- the exchange's
insider-trading feed lists the acquirer names, quantities and dates (confirmed live). The takeover code
(SAST, 2011) requires disclosure of \textbf{substantial acquisitions} of shares or voting rights past a
threshold. Structured metadata / PDF; \emph{not yet handled}.

\subsection{Tender offers and mergers}\label{sec:india-tender}
Control transactions: \textbf{open offers} under the takeover code (SAST) --- the Indian tender offer,
triggered when an acquirer crosses the control threshold --- and \textbf{schemes of arrangement} (mergers,
demergers) sanctioned by the National Company Law Tribunal and filed with the exchange. Offer documents and
scheme papers are PDF; \emph{not yet handled}.

\subsection{Issuance disclosures}\label{sec:india-issuance}
Raising capital: the offer documents for \textbf{public and rights issues} under the capital-issue
regulations (ICDR, 2018) --- the draft red-herring prospectus and the prospectus --- and \textbf{buy-backs}.
These are among the richest PDF documents in the universe; \emph{not yet handled} (a PDF-track candidate).

\subsection{Result format templates by company class}\label{sec:india-formats}
Unlike a uniform IFRS regime (or K-IFRS at DART, \S\ref{sec:dart-forms}), \textbf{SEBI does not prescribe one
results format: it prescribes a different format per \emph{company class}}, and the repository serves each as
a \emph{distinct} XBRL template, identified by the instance's \textbf{filename prefix}
(Table~\ref{tab:india-formats}). A general company's \texttt{INDAS\_} template reports
\texttt{RevenueFromOperations}; a bank's \texttt{BANKING\_} template has \emph{no such concept}, reporting
\texttt{InterestEarned} / \texttt{InterestExpended}, \texttt{Deposits} and \texttt{Advances} (the Banking
Regulation Act shape); an NBFC files \texttt{NBFC\_INDAS\_}. The adapter must branch on the prefix --- a
general-format classifier or oracle silently misses a bank's income statement. India's forms are genuinely
its own; they do not map onto another jurisdiction's shapes.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{The SEBI results formats \texttt{EDGAR.jl} classifies, across the \emph{two} India taxonomy
families --- each a distinct XBRL template, keyed by the instance filename prefix. ``bundled'' = the
format's presentation, label \emph{and} calculation linkbases ship with \texttt{EDGAR.jl} for full offline
classification, labels and roll-up checks; ``verified'' = confirmed against a real filing.}
\label{tab:india-formats}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.26\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}p{0.28\textwidth}p{0.13\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Template (prefix)} & \textbf{Company class} & \textbf{Characteristic top-line concepts} & \textbf{Status} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textbf{\texttt{in-bse-fin}} --- the \texttt{corporates-financial-results} feed (\texttt{NSEFilings}), frozen $\sim$FY2024} \\
\midrule
\texttt{INDAS\_} & General (Ind-AS) & \texttt{RevenueFromOperations}; Ind-AS P\&L, BS, cash-flow, segment & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{BANKING\_} & Banks & \texttt{InterestEarned} / \texttt{InterestExpended}; \texttt{Deposits}, \texttt{Advances} & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{NBFC\_INDAS\_} & Non-banking financial companies & Ind-AS on the NBFC schedule & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{(schemaRef-routed)} & Other than Banks (legacy) & pre-Ind-AS results shape & bundled; unvalidated \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textbf{\texttt{in-capmkt}} --- SEBI \textbf{Integrated Filing} (\texttt{IntegratedFilings}), the current framework, all sectors} \\
\midrule
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_INDAS\_} & General (Ind-AS) & \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_BANKING\_} & Banks & \texttt{InterestEarned}, \texttt{Deposits}, \texttt{Advances} & bundled \\
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_NBFC\_INDAS\_} & NBFCs & Ind-AS on the NBFC schedule & bundled \\
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_LI\_} & Life insurers & \texttt{GrossPremiumIncome}, \texttt{NetSurplusDeficit} & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_GI\_} & General insurers & \texttt{GrossPremiumsWritten}, \texttt{UnderwritingProfitOrLoss} & verified; bundled \\
\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_RESULTIR\_} & REITs / InvITs & \texttt{RevenueFromOperations}, \texttt{NetDistributableCashFlows} & verified; bundled \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Insurers and REITs --- the \texttt{in-capmkt} Integrated Filing seam (handled).} The
\texttt{corporates-financial-results} feed above is the \emph{old} channel, frozen at $\sim$FY2024; SEBI's
current \textbf{Integrated Filing} framework serves \emph{every} sector on a \emph{different} taxonomy,
\textbf{\texttt{in-capmkt}} (\texttt{www.sebi.gov.in/xbrl/2025-01-31/in-capmkt}) --- including insurers and
REITs/InvITs, which the old feed never carried. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} handles it as a second discovery source,
\texttt{IntegratedFilings} (\S\ref{sec:india-retrieve}), over the \texttt{integrated-filing-results}
endpoint, with a dedicated \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} and bundled \texttt{in-capmkt} presentation/label/
calculation linkbases (one per sector). Life (\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_LI\_}) and general
(\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_GI\_}) insurers report a premium-driven revenue account
(\texttt{GrossPremiumIncome} / \texttt{GrossPremiumsWritten}) with no \texttt{in-bse-fin} equivalent, verified
against SBI~Life, HDFC~Life and ICICI~Lombard. \textbf{REITs/InvITs} are the one sector that stayed on the
\emph{old} \texttt{corporates-financial-results} endpoint --- reached with \texttt{index=reitsinvits} (not the
integrated-filing endpoint, which returns nothing for them) --- but file the Integrated Filing taxonomy all
the same, as \texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_RESULTIR\_} XBRL on \texttt{in-capmkt} \emph{2026-01-31}; verified
against Embassy~REIT and IndiGrid~InvIT. Their P\&L uses the general Ind-AS concepts
(\texttt{RevenueFromOperations}), so \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} already anchors it, with
\texttt{NetDistributableCashFlows} landing in the cash-flow statement.

\subsection{Taxonomies}\label{sec:india-taxonomies}
Each family's formats share a national namespace but at a \emph{version} that varies by template. Within
\texttt{in-bse-fin} the general Ind-AS template binds \texttt{.../2020-03-31/in-bse-fin}, the banking
template \texttt{.../2019-09-30/in-bse-fin}; within \texttt{in-capmkt} the sector templates bind
\texttt{.../2025-01-31/in-capmkt} (REITs/InvITs, \texttt{.../2026-01-31/in-capmkt}). So ``the Indian
taxonomy'' is really \emph{two} small families of BSE/SEBI-published templates, each sharing IFRS-identical
local names. Classification keys on the namespace --- not the version or the prefix --- so one
\texttt{vocab\_indas} spans every \texttt{in-bse-fin} format and one \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} spans every
\texttt{in-capmkt} format (\S\ref{sec:india-classify}); but code that resolves labels or the calculation
linkbase must use the exact version \emph{and} sector the instance declares (the linkbases are bundled
per-format, routed by the filename prefix).

\subsection{Statement types and classification}\label{sec:india-statements}\label{sec:india-classify}
A results filing is a \textbf{condensed} statement set, \emph{not} the full annual accounts. In the general
(\texttt{INDAS\_}) format it carries the \textbf{balance sheet} (\texttt{Assets},
\texttt{EquityAndLiabilities}), the \textbf{statement of profit and loss} including \textbf{other
comprehensive income} (\texttt{RevenueFromOperations}\,\ldots\,\texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod},
\texttt{ComprehensiveIncomeForThePeriod}), the \textbf{cash-flow statement} (indirect method ---
\texttt{TypeOfCashFlowStatement} $=$ \texttt{"Cash Flow Indirect"}) and \textbf{segment} reporting. It does
\emph{not} carry a statement of changes in equity, nor the notes --- those, with the full statements, live
in the separate annual report (Reg.~34, a PDF, out of scope). The \textbf{banking} format instead reports
interest earned / expended, deposits and advances; the \textbf{NBFC} format, Ind-AS on the NBFC schedule.
Every filing self-declares its shape through metadata concepts: \texttt{NatureOfReportStandaloneConsolidated},
\texttt{ReportingQuarter}, \texttt{WhetherResultsAreAuditedOrUnaudited}, \texttt{TypeOfCashFlowStatement},
\texttt{LevelOfRoundingUsedInFinancialStatements} (e.g.\ \texttt{"Crores"}) and
\texttt{DescriptionOfPresentationCurrency} (e.g.\ \texttt{"INR"}). An \emph{annual} results filing carries
both the current-quarter and the full-year figures, so \texttt{facts(f)} returns a 3-month \emph{and} a
12-month fact for each flow concept (just as an SEC 10-Q carries the quarter and the year-to-date); select
the one you want by its period, exactly as in any other jurisdiction.

\medskip\noindent Every format tags under a national namespace with IFRS-identical local names ---
\texttt{in-bse-fin} for the old feed, \texttt{in-capmkt} for Integrated Filing (\S\ref{sec:india-taxonomy}).
Because \texttt{EDGAR.jl} classifies by \emph{namespace}, not prefix, India is classified through two
dedicated national vocabularies --- \texttt{vocab\_indas.jl} keyed on \texttt{in-bse-fin} and
\texttt{vocab\_incapmkt.jl} keyed on \texttt{in-capmkt} --- the inverse of DART, which needed no vocabulary
at all (\S\ref{sec:dart-kifrs}). Each supplies the key-anchor concepts per format (the general Ind-AS lines,
the banking interest-based lines \emph{and} the insurers' premium lines); for the \emph{full} statement ---
every line item, not just the anchors --- \texttt{EDGAR.jl} bundles the BSE/SEBI \textbf{presentation
linkbase} (one per format, selected by the filename prefix), since the instances carry none and neither BSE
nor SEBI hosts them resolvably. Together they place the whole balance sheet, income statement and cash-flow
statement (verified: Reliance 243 of 294 facts classified, the rest being segment/dimensional). Two Ind-AS
presentation choices in the \emph{general}-format income statement --- revenue reported \emph{gross of
excise duty}, and a broad \emph{finance-costs} bucket --- are where our figures legitimately differ from
third-party sources; \S\ref{sec:india-yahoo} is the reconciliation map (for the general format; banks reconcile on
different lines).

\section{Retrieving filings}\label{sec:india-retrieve}

Discovery is \textbf{keyless}. \texttt{discover(NSEFilings();\ symbol=\ldots)} lists a listed company's
financial-results filings by its NSE ticker; each returned \texttt{FilingHandle} carries the Ind-AS XBRL URL,
which \texttt{fetch\_filing} downloads into the same \texttt{Filing} every other system produces. The whole
flow is three lines:

\begin{lstlisting}
hs = discover(NSEFilings(); symbol = "RELIANCE")   # annual, consolidated, audited (the defaults)
f  = fetch_filing(first(hs))                        # newest filing; downloads the Ind-AS XBRL
facts(f)                                            # the statements  (narratives(f) for the Reg. 30 notes)
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent A few notes:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{No key, no setup.} NSE's public JSON API sits behind bot protection that requires a browser
    session; \texttt{EDGAR.jl} bootstraps and refreshes that session automatically, so there is nothing to
    configure --- unlike EDINET, Companies House or DART, India needs no credential.
  \item \textbf{By ticker.} Discovery keys off the NSE \texttt{symbol} (e.g.\ \texttt{"RELIANCE"},
    \texttt{"TCS"}). The narrowing keywords are \texttt{period} (\texttt{"Annual"} or \texttt{"Quarterly"})
    and \texttt{consolidated} / \texttt{audited} (\texttt{true} / \texttt{false}, or \texttt{nothing} to
    keep both); the defaults return the annual, consolidated, audited filings.
  \item \textbf{Handles, newest first.} Each handle's identity is the filing's \textbf{ISIN}
    (\texttt{EntityId(:isin,\ "INE002A01018")}) and its \texttt{period\_end} the financial-year end, so
    \texttt{first(hs)} is the most recent; pick any other by period.
  \item \textbf{Same API as everywhere.} \texttt{fetch\_filing(h)} $\to$ \texttt{facts(f)} /
    \texttt{narratives(f)} / \texttt{facts(f;\ classify=true)} $\to$ \texttt{select\_statement} is identical
    to SEC, ESEF or DART --- nothing about working an Indian filing differs for the caller. Classification
    uses the dedicated \texttt{vocab\_indas} plus the bundled BSE presentation linkbase
    (\S\ref{sec:india-statements}).
\end{itemize}

\section{Reconciling income-statement figures with Yahoo Finance}\label{sec:india-yahoo}

India has no Arelle / \href{https://filings.xbrl.org}{filings.xbrl.org} oracle (the same gap as Korea,
\S\ref{sec:dart-oracle}), so the cheap independent cross-check on a filer's headline numbers is
\textbf{Yahoo Finance} (NSE tickers \texttt{.NS}, BSE \texttt{.BO}). But Yahoo's line-item \emph{names}, and
their \emph{scope}, are not the Ind-AS \texttt{in-bse-fin} concepts: a na\"ive value-against-value
comparison appears to ``fail'' on lines that are merely \emph{defined} differently, not mis-extracted. This
section is the map --- for each income-statement line, the XBRL concept, the Yahoo field, and how to get
from one to the other. It was verified line-by-line on three FY2024 consolidated filings: Reliance
Industries, Infosys and Indian~Oil. All three are \textbf{general-format} (\texttt{INDAS\_}) filers
(\S\ref{sec:india-forms}); a bank's \texttt{BANKING\_} results reconcile on different lines (interest
earned/expended, not revenue) and are not covered here.

\paragraph{Rule of thumb.} \textbf{Every profit line from profit-before-tax downward matches Yahoo to the
rupee} (tax, profit for the period, owners' share, minority interest --- and depreciation). The two lines
that carry Indian-GAAP-specific gross-ups --- \textbf{revenue} (excise duty) and \textbf{finance costs}
(lease interest, unwinding of discount) --- are where the two sources legitimately diverge. Anchor a
validation on the profit lines; treat revenue and finance-costs as \emph{definitional-difference} lines.

\subsection{The concept map}
Each income-statement line, as \texttt{in-bse-fin:} concept $\leftrightarrow$ Yahoo field (prefixes dropped):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Revenue:} \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} vs Yahoo \texttt{TotalRevenue} ---
    \emph{Yahoo $=$ XBRL $-$ excise duty}; equal when there is no excise (deep dive below).
  \item \textbf{Finance / interest cost:} \texttt{FinanceCosts} vs Yahoo \texttt{InterestExpense} --- the
    XBRL bucket is a \emph{superset} (adds lease-liability interest and unwinding of discount), so
    XBRL~$\ge$~Yahoo (deep dive below).
  \item \textbf{Depreciation:} \texttt{DepreciationDepletionAndAmortisationExpense} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{ReconciledDepreciation} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Profit before tax:} \texttt{ProfitBeforeTax} vs Yahoo \texttt{PretaxIncome} --- Yahoo may
    take \texttt{SegmentProfitBeforeTax}, which \emph{adds share of profit of associates/JVs} (Ind-AS shows
    that post-tax): \texttt{PretaxIncome $=$ ProfitBeforeTax $+$ ShareOfProfit\ldots Associates}.
  \item \textbf{Tax:} \texttt{TaxExpense} vs Yahoo \texttt{TaxProvision} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Profit for the period:} \texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{NetIncomeContinuousOperations} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Net income (owners):} \texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToOwnersOfParent} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{NetIncome} / \texttt{NetIncomeCommonStockholders} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Non-controlling interest:} \texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToNonControllingInterests} vs
    Yahoo \texttt{MinorityInterests} --- \emph{equal} (Yahoo carries it negative).
  \item \textbf{Basic EPS:} \texttt{BasicEarningsLossPerShareFromContinuingAndDiscontinuedOperations} vs
    Yahoo \texttt{BasicEPS} --- Yahoo \emph{restates for later bonus issues / splits} (Reliance
    \texttt{102.90} $\to$ Yahoo \texttt{51.45} after the 1:1 bonus).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Revenue --- excise duty (\texttt{RevenueFromOperations} $-$ excise $=$ \texttt{TotalRevenue})}
Ind-AS reports \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} \textbf{gross of excise duty}, with the excise shown as a
separate expense line --- tagged \texttt{in-bse-fin:OtherExpenses} in the operating-expense breakdown member
whose \texttt{DescriptionOfOtherExpenses} is \texttt{"Excise Duty"}. Yahoo (like most data vendors) presents
revenue \textbf{net of excise}. Because the excise sits in \emph{both} revenue and expenses, it cancels at
profit-before-tax --- which is why every profit line still matches. Post-GST (2017) excise survives only on
\textbf{petroleum} (petrol, diesel, ATF, crude, still outside GST), so this affects refiners and not
services or most goods companies. Table~\ref{tab:india-rev-recon} works it across the three filers.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Revenue reconciliation, FY2024 (Rs crore). \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} $-$ excise duty
($-$ other operating revenue) $=$ Yahoo \texttt{TotalRevenue}.}
\label{tab:india-rev-recon}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.22\textwidth}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Filer} & \textbf{Rev.\ from ops} & \textbf{$-$ Excise} & \textbf{$-$ Other op.\ rev.} & \textbf{$=$ Yahoo} \\
\midrule
Reliance Industries & 914{,}472 & 13{,}408 & --- & 901{,}064 \\
Indian Oil (IOC)    & 881{,}235 & 104{,}884 & $\approx$3{,}510 & 772{,}842 \\
Infosys             & 153{,}670 & --- (none) & --- & 153{,}674$^{*}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent For Reliance the excise line closes the gap \textbf{exactly} ($914{,}472 - 13{,}408 = 901{,}064$).
For Infosys (IT services) there is \emph{no} excise, so revenue matches. Indian~Oil is the honest edge case:
excise ($104{,}884$) explains 96.8\,\% of the gap, leaving a $\sim$Rs~3{,}510~crore residual that is
\textbf{not present anywhere in the condensed results XBRL} (its \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} is a single
undivided line). The most likely source is \emph{other operating revenue} netted out by the vendor but
itemised only in the full annual report --- a reminder that for a \emph{rupee-exact} revenue check the
quarterly results filing is sometimes insufficient, and one should anchor on profit-before-tax (excise- and
associates-neutral) instead. \footnotesize$^{*}$Yahoo reports Infosys in \textbf{USD} (\$18.562~bn); see
\emph{Caveats}.\normalsize

\subsection{Finance costs --- a broader bucket than Yahoo's interest expense}
Ind-AS \texttt{FinanceCosts} is a \emph{superset} of Yahoo's \texttt{InterestExpense}: besides interest on
borrowings it includes interest on \textbf{lease liabilities} (Ind-AS~116), \textbf{unwinding of discount}
on provisions (decommissioning / asset-retirement) and other borrowing costs, whereas Yahoo's
\texttt{InterestExpense} is the narrower pure-debt figure. So the XBRL value is consistently $\ge$ Yahoo's:
Reliance \texttt{23{,}118} vs \texttt{21{,}772}; Indian~Oil \texttt{7{,}826} vs \texttt{7{,}584} (Rs~crore).
It is a scope difference, not an error.

\subsection{The lines that match, and the associates reclassification}
Tax (\texttt{TaxExpense} $=$ \texttt{TaxProvision}), profit for the period (\texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod} $=$
\texttt{NetIncomeContinuousOperations}), owners' net income
(\texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToOwnersOfParent} $=$ \texttt{NetIncome}), minority interest and
depreciation all match \textbf{to the rupee} across the filers. The one apparent exception, profit-before-tax,
is a \emph{classification} difference: Indian~Oil's \texttt{ProfitBeforeTax} is \texttt{55{,}742} but Yahoo's
\texttt{PretaxIncome} is \texttt{57{,}288} --- because Yahoo takes \texttt{SegmentProfitBeforeTax}, which
includes the Rs~1{,}546~crore \emph{share of profit of associates} that Ind-AS presents \emph{after} tax.
It reconciles ($55{,}742 + 1{,}546 = 57{,}288$) and washes out by net income.

\subsection{Two caveats: currency and per-share restatement}
\textbf{Currency.} Yahoo does \emph{not} always report an Indian issuer in rupees. Companies that publish
USD financial statements are carried by Yahoo in \textbf{USD} --- Infosys's FY2024 income statement comes
back as \$18.562~bn revenue, \$3.167~bn net income --- so the currency must be \emph{detected}, not assumed
INR, and converted at the year's average rate (Infosys FY2024 $\approx$ Rs~82.79/USD, a rate the
depreciation line confirms: \$565~m $\times$ 82.79 $=$ Rs~4{,}678~crore, matching our figure exactly).
Reliance and Indian~Oil are reported in INR. \textbf{Per-share restatement.} Yahoo restates EPS for
\emph{later} bonus issues and splits, so a filed \texttt{BasicEarningsLossPerShare} will not match Yahoo's
\texttt{BasicEPS} across a corporate action (Reliance's filed \texttt{102.90} vs Yahoo's \texttt{51.45}
after the 2024 1:1 bonus).

\chapter{Russia \textemdash{} CBR / e-disclosure}
\label{sys:russia}

\emph{Status: planned --- placeholder chapter, not yet researched or implemented.}

Russian corporate disclosure centres on the \textbf{Bank of Russia} (CBR) as securities-market regulator and
the authorised \textbf{information-disclosure agencies} (the e-disclosure centres, e.g.\ Interfax's system),
with issuers reporting under \textbf{RAS} (Russian Accounting Standards) and, for consolidated accounts,
IFRS. The repository survey --- which venues expose machine-readable (XBRL) filings versus PDF, the access
model, and any sanctions/access constraints from outside Russia --- is \textbf{not yet done}; this chapter is
a scaffold to be filled once India is complete and Russia is picked up.

\chapter{Additional Data Sources}
\label{sec:validation}

How do you know the numbers \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts are right? You can check them against
independent references --- ``trust, but verify.'' \texttt{EDGAR.jl} ships four \emph{do-it-yourself}
data sources under \texttt{src/sources/}, each an independent reference you compare the extraction
against. They are deliberately \textbf{not part of the \texttt{EDGAR} module} (not \texttt{include}d by
\texttt{src/EDGAR.jl}), so they add \textbf{no dependencies} to the package and never touch the working
code; you load one on demand when you want reassurance. A stray \texttt{.jl} file under \texttt{src/}
is ignored by the package loader.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{The four additional data sources (validation oracles).}
\label{tab:oracles}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.26\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}p{0.12\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Reference} & \textbf{Kind} & \textbf{Coverage} & \textbf{Extra deps} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Yahoo Finance} (via \texttt{YFinance.jl}) & \emph{data} check --- independent pipeline; headline totals within 1\% & global (100+ countries) & \texttt{YFinance} \\
\textbf{AlphaVantage} (official REST API) & \emph{data} check --- independent pipeline; headline totals within 1\% & US-strong (SEC filers) & none \\
filings.xbrl.org \textbf{xBRL-JSON}, produced by \textbf{Arelle} (the reference XBRL processor) & \emph{parser} check --- same document, independent parser; fact-level parity & ESEF + UKSEF (EU, UK) & none \\
\textbf{Arelle run locally} (the reference XBRL processor, as a subprocess) & \emph{parser} check --- same document, fact-level \textbf{incl.\ dimensions} (covers the SCE) & \textbf{any} XBRL package (incl.\ KR, JP) & Arelle CLI \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent They answer two different questions. A \textbf{data} oracle (\textbf{Yahoo Finance},
\textbf{AlphaVantage}) confirms the \emph{numbers match the outside world}: it compares the headline
totals (Assets, Equity, Revenue, Net income, operating cash flow) against an entirely separate data
pipeline, across all three statements. A \textbf{parser} oracle (\textbf{Arelle}) confirms we
\emph{read the filing right}: it compares the extracted facts against the \emph{same filing} as parsed by
the reference XBRL processor. There are two: \texttt{arelle\_esef\_oracle.jl} fetches Arelle's
\emph{already-published} xBRL-JSON from filings.xbrl.org (no dependency, but limited to the ESEF/UKSEF
filings the aggregator hosts and to consolidated facts), while \texttt{arelle\_local\_oracle.jl} \emph{runs}
Arelle locally on the filing itself (needs the Arelle CLI, but then validates \emph{any} filing --- SEC,
ESEF/UKSEF, Korea/DART, Japan --- and \emph{every} fact, dimensions included, so it covers the statement of
changes in equity). It accepts whatever shape a filing comes in:

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item a \textbf{bare inline-XBRL/XBRL instance} --- e.g.\ a SEC filing, whose \texttt{f.url} is the inline
    \texttt{.htm} (not a ZIP); Arelle is pointed at the URL (or a local file) and fetches the DTS itself;
  \item a formal \textbf{report package} (ESEF/UKSEF, carrying \texttt{META-INF/taxonomyPackage.xml}) ---
    handed to Arelle \emph{as a package} so its catalog remaps the standard taxonomy;
  \item a \textbf{loose package} (DART \texttt{fnlttXbrl}, an EDINET submission) --- extracted, with Arelle
    pointed at the instance.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Both oracles compare values \emph{raw} (the as-stored value; the classified value flips sign for
\texttt{negatedLabel}/cash-outflow concepts). The local oracle is verified at \textbf{100\%} on the five
financial statements (balance sheet, income statement, comprehensive income, cash flow and changes in
equity) across SEC (Apple 10-K), ESEF (Citycon, 312/312), UKSEF (Kainos) and DART (Samsung, 5997/5997);
the few unconfirmed facts are note-level non-financial disclosures (counts, percentages). A parser oracle
cannot catch a filer \emph{mis-tagging} a value (everyone reads the same wrong tag); a data oracle can, but
only approximately, since the data providers normalize.

\medskip\noindent This is the same kind of check the package already applies on the SEC side: the
extracted values are validated against the SEC's \texttt{companyfacts} API (the SEC's own parse of the
same filing). Each filing system has an independent reference of this sort --- which is what lets us
trust the one jurisdiction-agnostic parser across systems where no such API exists
(\S\ref{sec:architecture}). Each oracle's \texttt{validate} returns a row table --- one row per fact
(or per headline metric) with the \texttt{EDGAR} value, the reference value, the relative difference
and a \texttt{match} flag --- so you can \texttt{filter} to mismatches or render it with
\texttt{PrettyTables}. A \texttt{User-Agent} must be set first (\S\ref{sec:ua}); the scripts fall
back to the \texttt{EDGAR\_UA} environment variable.

\section{Yahoo Finance}

A \emph{data} oracle with the broadest reach: Yahoo Finance covers 100+ countries, so it cross-checks
SEC and ESEF filers alike. It compares the headline totals against Yahoo's standardized fundamentals
within a 1\% tolerance (Yahoo rounds). It needs \texttt{YFinance.jl} in the active environment --- a
dependency that is \emph{earned}, because Yahoo has no official API: its endpoints are undocumented and
cookie/crumb-gated, and \texttt{YFinance.jl} encapsulates that fragile access.

\paragraph{Validating the extraction.} Extract a filer with \texttt{EDGAR}, then cross-check its
headline totals against Yahoo:

\begin{lstlisting}
using YFinance
include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "yahoo_oracle.jl"))
using .YahooOracle

# 1. EDGAR side -- Apple's latest 10-K, classified (the figures under test).
f = fetch_filing("320193", first(filings_by_cik("320193"; forms = "10-K")).accession)
select_statement(facts(f; classify = true), "BalanceSheet")     # eyeball EDGAR's numbers

# 2. Cross-check the five headline totals against Yahoo (an independent pipeline).
v = YahooOracle.validate(:sec, "320193", "AAPL")   # CIK or LEI + the Yahoo ticker
filter(r -> !r.match && r.yahoo !== nothing, v)    # empty => all agree within 1%
YahooOracle.report()                               # or the built-in multi-firm panel
\end{lstlisting}

\paragraph{Why a \texttt{ticker}?} A \texttt{Filing} knows the filer's \texttt{EntityId} (CIK or LEI),
not its exchange symbol, so the data oracles take the ticker explicitly (\texttt{"AAPL"},
\texttt{"CTY1S.HE"}). The same applies to AlphaVantage below.

\section{AlphaVantage}

A second \emph{data} oracle, strongest for US (SEC) filers. Unlike Yahoo it needs \textbf{no extra
dependency}: AlphaVantage is an official, documented REST API (one keyed GET returns clean JSON), so
the oracle uses \texttt{EDGAR}'s own cached fetcher directly. That caching matters --- the free key
allows only \(\sim\)25 calls/day, so the on-disk response cache makes re-runs free, and a fresh run is
paced under the \(\sim\)5/minute limit (a burst of four network calls per minute; cached calls never
block). Set the free key once (it falls back to the \texttt{ALPHA\_VANTAGE\_API\_KEY} environment
variable):

\begin{lstlisting}[style=term]
# https://www.alphavantage.co/support/#api-key
% export ALPHA_VANTAGE_API_KEY="your-key"
% julia --project=. src/sources/alphavantage_oracle.jl   # the built-in US panel
\end{lstlisting}

\paragraph{Validating the extraction.} Extract a US filer with \texttt{EDGAR}, then cross-check against
AlphaVantage's fundamentals:

\begin{lstlisting}
include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "alphavantage_oracle.jl"))
using .AlphaVantageOracle

# 1. EDGAR side -- Microsoft's latest 10-K, classified.
f = fetch_filing("789019", first(filings_by_cik("789019"; forms = "10-K")).accession)
select_statement(facts(f; classify = true), "IncomeStatement")

# 2. Cross-check against AlphaVantage (cached; a fresh run is paced under the free tier).
v = AlphaVantageOracle.validate(:sec, "789019", "MSFT")   # CIK + the AlphaVantage symbol
filter(r -> !r.match && r.alphavantage !== nothing, v)    # real disagreements only
AlphaVantageOracle.report()                               # or the built-in US panel
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent On the default panel the balance-sheet totals match the extraction to \texttt{0.000\%}.
A metric AlphaVantage did not return (e.g. a throttled call) has \texttt{alphavantage === nothing} and
is \emph{not} counted as a mismatch --- hence the \texttt{!== nothing} filter above.
Throttle responses (which AlphaVantage returns with HTTP~200) are purged from the cache so they cannot
poison a later run.

\section{Arelle}

\textbf{Arelle} is the reference, open-source XBRL processor --- an independent implementation of the
standard. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} validates against it two ways, both \emph{parser} oracles (same document,
fact-level): the \textbf{local} oracle runs Arelle itself on the filing and covers \emph{every}
\texttt{FilingSystem} and \emph{every} fact; the \textbf{published-JSON} oracle reuses Arelle's output from
filings.xbrl.org with no extra dependency, for the ESEF/UKSEF filings that aggregator hosts.

\subsection{Local Arelle --- the universal oracle}

\texttt{arelle\_local\_oracle.jl} runs the Arelle CLI (\texttt{arelleCmdLine}) on the downloaded filing and
compares EDGAR.jl's value for \emph{every} fact --- keyed by concept + period + full \emph{dimension} set
--- against Arelle's. Because it keys on dimensions, it validates the dimensional statements the headline
data checks cannot, in particular the \textbf{statement of changes in equity}. It needs the Arelle CLI
installed (located via the \texttt{ARELLE\_CMD} env var, \texttt{PATH}, or the macOS app), and accepts
whatever shape a filing arrives in:

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item a \textbf{bare inline-XBRL/XBRL instance} (SEC --- \texttt{f.url} is the inline \texttt{.htm}, not a
    ZIP); Arelle is pointed at the URL/file and fetches the DTS itself;
  \item a formal \textbf{report package} (ESEF/UKSEF, with \texttt{META-INF/taxonomyPackage.xml}) --- handed
    to Arelle \emph{as a package} so its catalog remaps the standard taxonomy;
  \item a \textbf{loose package} (DART \texttt{fnlttXbrl}, an EDINET submission) --- extracted, with Arelle
    pointed at the instance.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Values are compared \textbf{raw} (as stored): EDGAR.jl's classified value flips sign for
\texttt{negatedLabel}/cash-outflow concepts, so the classification is used only to \emph{label} each row by
statement, never to compare. The oracle is verified at \textbf{100\,\% on the financial statements ---
including the changes-in-equity statement --- across every \texttt{FilingSystem}}: SEC (Apple 10-K), ESEF
(Citycon, 312/312), UKSEF (Kainos), DART (Samsung, 5997/5997) and EDINET (Toyota 1378/1378; Nintendo, MUFG
and Canon also 100\,\%). The handful of unconfirmed facts are note-level non-financial disclosures (counts,
percentages).

\begin{lstlisting}
include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "arelle_local_oracle.jl"))
using .ArelleLocalOracle

# Any filing, any system: materialise it, run Arelle, compare every fact (incl. the SCE).
f = fetch_filing(EDINET(), "Toyota-annual.zip")
ArelleLocalOracle.report(ArelleLocalOracle.validate(f))      # per-statement parity
filter(r -> !r.match, ArelleLocalOracle.validate(f))         # any discrepancies

# DART convenience -- discover + validate in one call:
set_credentials(DART(); api_key = ENV["DART_API_KEY"])
ArelleLocalOracle.report_dart("005930"; year = 2023)         # Samsung -- 5997/5997, incl. SCE
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Published xBRL-JSON --- ESEF/UKSEF, no dependency}

\texttt{arelle\_esef\_oracle.jl} skips the local install: it fetches the \emph{same filing's} xBRL-JSON as
\textbf{already published} by \href{https://filings.xbrl.org}{filings.xbrl.org} (produced by Arelle), using
just the HTTP+JSON \texttt{EDGAR} already has. So it adds no dependency, but is limited to the ESEF + UKSEF
filings the aggregator hosts and to consolidated (undimensioned) facts. It mirrors
\href{https://github.com/trr266/ESEF.jl}{\texttt{ESEF.jl}}'s \texttt{pluck\_xbrl\_json} (MIT, TRR~266) ---
credit there.

\begin{lstlisting}[style=term]
# the built-in firm panel (exits non-zero if parity drops below 95%):
% julia --project=. src/sources/arelle_esef_oracle.jl
\end{lstlisting}

\paragraph{Validating the extraction.} Discover and extract an ESEF/UKSEF filer with \texttt{EDGAR}, then
check every consolidated fact against the same filing's xBRL-JSON:

\begin{lstlisting}
include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "arelle_esef_oracle.jl"))
using .ArelleEsefOracle
filter(r -> !r.match, ArelleEsefOracle.validate("549300P8N0P6KDGTJ206"; year = 2023))  # discrepancies
ArelleEsefOracle.statements("549300P8N0P6KDGTJ206")    # parity per BS / IS / CF (e.g. 100%)
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent \texttt{ArelleEsefOracle.statements(lei)} reports parity \emph{per statement} --- one row
\texttt{(statement, matched, total)} for \texttt{BalanceSheet}, \texttt{IncomeStatement} and
\texttt{CashFlow} --- near-exact on the default panel (\textbf{100\,\% fact parity}).



\chapter{Related Work and Comparison}
\label{sec:related-work}

EDGAR.jl enters a mature ecosystem of EDGAR/XBRL tooling, mostly in Python and R. It is
worth being explicit about where it follows the field, where it differs, and what it does
not (yet) do.

\paragraph{The landscape.} In Python, \href{https://github.com/dgunning/edgartools}{\texttt{edgartools}}
is the modern leader --- it turns filings into typed objects and is especially strong on XBRL
statements, with two ideas we admire: \emph{concept standardization} (mapping each issuer's tag
to a common concept for cross-company comparison) and \emph{statement stitching} (assembling a
multi-period statement across filings). It is, however, in-memory --- it exports to
\texttt{DataFrame}s rather than persisting a warehouse. \href{https://arelle.org/}{Arelle} is the
reference open-source XBRL engine --- the SEC's own EDGAR Renderer is built on it --- and its
\texttt{xbrlDB} plug-in persists facts using the canonical \emph{XBRL-US Public Database} schema.
Tools such as \texttt{sec-edgar-downloader} and \texttt{sec-downloader} fetch raw filings and
leave parsing to you. In R, \texttt{finstr} (on top of the \texttt{XBRL} package) organises
instances into income statements, balance sheets and cash-flow statements. Julia, by contrast,
has had no established EDGAR/XBRL library.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{EDGAR.jl among representative EDGAR/XBRL tools.}
\label{tab:related-work}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.06\textwidth}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.15\textwidth}p{0.10\textwidth}p{0.09\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Tool} & \textbf{Lang} & \textbf{Focus} & \textbf{Persistent warehouse} & \textbf{Concept std.} & \textbf{Picker / prov.} \\
\midrule
\textbf{EDGAR.jl} & Julia & Pick a region $\to$ four-layer export + fact warehouse & DuckDB (embedded) & yes (opt-in) & \textbf{yes} \\
edgartools & Python & Typed filings; XBRL statements & in-memory & yes & no \\
Arelle & Python & Reference XBRL engine / renderer & Postgres, RDF, \ldots & --- & no \\
sec-downloader & Python & Download raw filings & no & no & no \\
finstr + XBRL & R & XBRL $\to$ IS / BS / CF & no & partial & no \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{What EDGAR.jl does differently.} Three things set it apart. \emph{(1)~An interactive
picker with provenance:} you select a region in the rendered filing, and every extracted fact
carries a \texttt{source\_selector} back to the exact DOM node --- a bridge between document
review, iXBRL semantics and relational analytics that the other tools, which extract purely
programmatically, do not offer. \emph{(2)~An embedded DuckDB warehouse:} zero-server and
single-file, where Arelle persists only to heavy databases and edgartools does not persist at
all --- and facts from any source share one table under a semantic key
(\S\ref{sec:export-layers}). \emph{(3)~A four-layer export} from a single selection --- lossless
iXBRL, Markdown, Facts JSON and DuckDB --- with provenance threaded throughout. And it is
Julia/Tables.jl-native, filling a gap in that ecosystem.

\paragraph{Authoritative enrichment.} A second distinction is \emph{where} EDGAR.jl's enrichment
comes from: a fact's statement is read from the filing's own presentation linkbase
(\texttt{statement\_map}, \S\ref{sec:export-layers}) and its roll-up arithmetic from the
calculation linkbase (\texttt{calculations}), rather than inferred --- and concept standardization
is pluggable (\texttt{set\_standardizer}), defaulting to \emph{none} and offering the bundled,
attributed edgartools mapping opt-in, instead of baking one opinionated mapping in.

\paragraph{What it does not (yet) do.} In fairness, the established tools still lead in breadth.
edgartools covers many more form types (Form 3/4/5, 13F, ADV, \dots) and now ships IFRS mappings,
while Arelle implements the full XBRL specification --- formula, dimensional validation, table
linkbases --- that EDGAR.jl does not. EDGAR.jl focuses on us-gaap financial-statement facts, and
its standardization is the bundled edgartools concept mapping rather than that project's newer,
data-driven one. The gaps an earlier draft named --- concept standardization, statement
classification, and the calculation linkbase --- are now closed
(\texttt{set\_standardizer(:edgartools)}, \texttt{statement\_map}, \texttt{calculations}).

\paragraph{Validation from the reference schema.} Reassuringly, the design we arrived at matches
the XBRL-US reference: it hashes each fact by its context so identical contexts collapse (our
semantic \texttt{fact\_key}), and assigns a fiscal year and period to every fact (our
\texttt{fy}/\texttt{fp}/\texttt{frame}). Where we diverge is deliberate --- the reference
normalises context, unit and dimension into separate tables for transactional Postgres, whereas
EDGAR.jl keeps a \emph{denormalised} wide facts table, which is what an analytical, columnar
store like DuckDB prefers.


% ===========================================================================
\appendix

\chapter{Glossary}
\label{sec:glossary}

SEC filings and their structured data come with their own vocabulary.
The terms that matter here:

\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.4em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
  \item[CIK] \emph{Central Index Key}, the SEC's unique ID for a filer,
    zero-padded to 10 digits (Apple $\to$ \texttt{0000320193}). Functions accept it
    as an integer or a string, with or without leading zeros (\texttt{320193}
    $\equiv$ \texttt{0000320193}). Look one up from a ticker or company name with
    \texttt{cik}.
  \item[Filing] a document submitted to the SEC, identified by an
    \textbf{accession number} and a \textbf{form type} (\texttt{10-K} annual
    report, \texttt{10-Q} quarterly, \texttt{8-K} current report, \dots). Get a filer's filings with \texttt{filings\_by\_cik}.
  \item[XBRL] \emph{eXtensible Business Reporting Language}, the machine-readable format companies tag their financial statements in; it is what makes the numbers queryable rather than locked inside a document. A pure XBRL filing is an XML document whose primary purpose is to represent structured facts,
  \begin{lstlisting}
<us-gaap:Assets contextRef="CurrentYear" unitRef="USD">
    1000000
</us-gaap:Assets>
\end{lstlisting}
A browser won't render this as a nice financial statement. It's intended for software.
\item[iXBRL] \emph{Inline-XBRL}: those same tags embedded directly inside the filing's human-readable HTML, so one document is both readable and
machine-parseable. The SEC has required it for financial statements since
around 2019. An iXBRL filing is an HTML document that looks like a normal report,
\begin{lstlisting}
<p>
Total assets were
<ix:nonFraction
    name="us-gaap:Assets"
    contextRef="CurrentYear"
    unitRef="USD">
    1000000
</ix:nonFraction>.
</p>
\end{lstlisting}
Historically, iXBRL was defined on top of XHTML (XML-compliant HTML), although modern tools often treat it as HTML with XML namespaces.
  \item[Fact] a single reported number: one \emph{concept}, for one
    \emph{period}, in one \emph{unit} (e.g.\ ``net income for FY2023 was
    96{,}995{,}000{,}000 USD''). \texttt{company\_facts} returns every fact a
    company has reported.
  \item[Concept] the \emph{name} of a reported line item, drawn from a taxonomy
    (e.g.\ \texttt{NetIncomeLoss}, \texttt{Assets}, \texttt{Revenues}) --- ``what is
    being measured''. \texttt{company\_concept} returns one concept over time.
  \item[Taxonomy] a dictionary that defines concepts. The big one is
    \textbf{\texttt{us-gaap}} (US accounting standards); \textbf{\texttt{dei}}
    (\emph{Document and Entity Information}) holds metadata about the filing
    itself; \textbf{\texttt{srt}} holds shared elements; \textbf{\texttt{ifrs-full}}
    is for filers reporting under IFRS; and each company has its own
    \textbf{extension} namespace (e.g.\ \texttt{aapl} for Apple-specific concepts).
  \item[Frame] a cross-section: one concept, one unit, one period, across
    \emph{every} filer that reported it. \texttt{xbrl\_frames} returns a frame.
  \item[Unit] the unit of measure of a fact: \texttt{USD}, \texttt{shares},
    \texttt{USD/shares} (per-share), \texttt{pure} (a ratio), \dots
  \item[Period] the time a fact covers, and how it is written. \textbf{Frames}
    use \emph{calendar} periods: \texttt{CY} + year, as in \texttt{CY2022} (a full
    calendar year), \texttt{CY2022Q4} (a quarter), or \texttt{CY2022Q4I} (a single
    instant --- note the trailing \texttt{I}). An \textbf{instant} is a point in
    time (balance-sheet items such as Assets, at quarter-end); a
    \textbf{duration} is a span (income-statement items such as Revenue). By
    contrast, \textbf{\texttt{FY}} in a company's own facts is its \emph{fiscal}
    year, which need not match the calendar (Apple's \texttt{FY2023} ended in
    September 2023).
\end{description}
\end{document}
